396:    The inaccuracies in Hartmann’s brief are typical of unverified, AI-generated
397:  filings, which have become an increasing concern for courts in recent years. While
398:  no federal or local rule explicitly prohibits the use of artificial intelligence in
399:  litigation, and Hartmann disclosed his use of generative AI in preparing his brief,
400:  see Pl.’s Resp. at 1 (acknowledging that Hartmann “used generative AI”), all
401:  parties—including those proceeding pro se—must fulfill their obligations under
402:  Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 11(b), which requires lawyers and unrepresented
403:  parties alike to certify that, after a reasonable inquiry, their legal contentions are
404:  warranted by existing law. Consequently, this duty necessitates a careful review of
405:  AI-generated statements and citations to ensure accuracy.

62:                                          II.     SANCTIONS
63: 
64:           As an initial matter, Defendant’s Motion appears to contain false citations and
65:  quotations. Specifically, Defendant provides at least one false quotation that the Court can
66:  nowhere identify in the cited case, nor any other case. Defendant also cites cases for propositions
67:  found nowhere in those cases. Such misquotations, miscitations, and misrepresentations often
68:  indicate the presence of artificial intelligence-generated hallucinations. It is a basic tenet of legal
69:  practice—and a rule of the State Bar of California—that a lawyer shall not “knowingly make a
70:  false statement of fact or law to a tribunal or fail to correct a false statement of material fact or law
71:  previously made to the tribunal by the lawyer.” Cal. Bar Rule 3.3. Further, under Federal Rule
72:  of Civil Procedure 11, “[b]y presenting to the court a pleading, written motion, or other paper . . .
73:  an attorney . . . certifies that to the best of the person’s knowledge, information, and belief,
74:  formed after an inquiry reasonable under the circumstances: (1) it is not being presented for any
75:  improper purpose, such as to harass, cause unnecessary delay, or needlessly increase the cost of
76:  litigation; [and] (2) the claims, defenses, and other legal contentions are warranted by existing
77:  law or by a nonfrivolous argument for extending, modifying, or reversing existing law or for
78:  establishing new law . . . .” Fed. R. Civ. Pro. 11.
